```text
Cudkowicz (PI) HEALEY ALS Platform Trial: Master Statistical Analysis Plan     Version 3.0, 06 Feb 2023


x   Baseline symptom severity (all ALSFRS-R questions scored 2 or greater vs. any question
    scored 0 or 1),
x   Early disease state (all ALSFRS-R questions scored 2 or greater, SVC 80%-predicted or
    greater, and time since symptom onset less than 24 months vs. not meeting all three criteria),
x   Site of symptom onset (bulbar vs. non-bulbar),
x   Baseline plasma NfL concentration (high vs. low by median split), and
x   Site (individual sites with at least 5 participants per treatment group and all participants from

For each subgroup, unknown, not reported, and missing will be considered as one group. All
individuals not included in a specified subgroup will be combined into a mixed, "other" group.
The "other" group will be included in analyses if its prevalence is greater than 5%; otherwise, the
"other" group will be excluded.
For each subgroup, the following estimates will be obtained:
x   the treatment-group specific estimate within each subgroup level,
x   the treatment difference within each subgroup level,
x   the difference of differences between each pair of subgroup levels, and
x   for subgroups with more than two levels, the F-test for the subgroup × treatment × time term
    will be estimated.
6.5.10 Comparison of Controls across Regimens
The magnitude of differences in regimen-specific rates of progression for primary and secondary
efficacy endpoints will be compared by random-slopes models for functional endpoints and by
Kaplan-Meier product-limit estimates, log-rank test, and Cox proportional hazards regression for
survival using the placebo participants in the FAS analysis set . The random-slopes models will
include fixed effects of regimen and regimen x time interaction, adjusting for time since onset,
delta-FRS, baseline log-transformed serum NfL level, baseline riluzole use, baseline edaravone
use, and baseline sodium phenylbutyrate/taurursodiol and their interactions with time. Fixed
terms other than regimen and month will be centered by subtracting the sample mean of a given
covariate. The models will include participant-specific intercepts and slopes with unstructured
covariance. Adjusted regimen-specific slope estimates, difference of the focal regimen control
group vs. each other regimen and vs. the average of all other regimens, and an overall F-test of
regimen-specific differences in rates of disease progression will be estimated. Regimen-specific
slopes will be plotted vs. calendar year of median randomization. Cox models will include main
effects for regimen, time since onset, delta-FRS, baseline log-transformed serum NfL level,
baseline riluzole use, baseline edaravone use and baseline sodium phenylbutyrate/taurursodiol
use. Adjusted regimen-specific hazard ratios of the focal regimen control group vs. each other
regimen and vs. the average of all other regimens, and an overall F-test of regimen-specific
differences in hazard of mortality will be estimated.
```

```text
Cudkowicz (PI) HEALEY ALS Platform Trial: Master Statistical Analysis Plan      Version 3.0, 06 Feb 2023


6.6     Safety Analyses
6.6.1    Treatment-emergent Adverse Events
TEAEs are defined as those adverse events with onset dates in the interval from treatment
initiation to either the Final Safety Visit if completed, the date of death if the participant dies, the
date of last study contact if the participant early terminates or is lost to follow-up, or the date of
first dose of study drug during participation in the ATE, if so exposed. Adverse events with onset
on the day of placebo-controlled treatment initiation and adverse events with incompletely
specified onset date where the ambiguous date spans the day of double-blind treatment initiation
will be assumed to be treatment emergent except for those known to precede first exposure to
study drug.
The incidence of TEAEs will be summarized by the number of events of a given classification
experienced by participants in each treatment group and by the number and proportion of
participants experiencing such an event in each treatment group in all safety samples. The
number of events and proportion of participants experiencing TEAEs will be summarized
without regard to MedDRA term for any TEAE, any serious TEAE, any severe TEAE, any fatal
TEAE, any related TEAE, and any TEAE leading to discontinuation of study drug. TEAEs will
be summarized by MedDRA system organ class, high level term, and preferred term for all
TEAEs. TEAEs, serious TEAEs, and non-serious TEAEs observed in at least 5% of participants
in a given treatment group will be summarized by MedDRA system organ class and preferred
term and by MedDRA preferred term alone. TEAEs will be summarized by MedDRA system
organ class and preferred term for serious TEAEs, severe TEAEs, fatal TEAEs, related TEAEs,
and TEAEs leading to discontinuation of study drug. TEAEs and serious TEAEs will be
stratified by the following subgroups: age, sex, race, ethnicity, baseline riluzole use, baseline
edaravone use, baseline sodium phenylbutyrate/taurursodiol use, weight, BMI, and CKD stage.
Subgroup classifications will be the same as described in Section 6.5.9 Subgroup Analyses
except that the "other" group will be retained regardless of prevalence.
For overall summaries of TEAE not classified by MedDRA term and for summaries of TEAEs,
serious TEAEs, and non-serious TEAEs observed in at least 5% of participants in a given
treatment group, treatment-specific TEAE incidence rates in units of number per 100 participant
years and treatment-dependent differences will be estimated by Poisson regression with log link
and offsets equal to the log of weeks from initiation to discontinuation of placebo-controlled
treatment. Comparison-wise 95% confidence intervals will be calculated with variance estimates
obtained by the delta method.
The incidence of TEAEs will be summarized without regard to MedDRA term in all safety
samples for the following characteristics: (a) seriousness, (b) severity, (c) relationship to study
drug, (d) action taken with study drug, (e) use of a concomitant medication, and (f) outcome. For
each level of a given TEAE characteristic, summaries will include the number of events of a
given classification and the number and proportion of participants for which that level of a
characteristic was the worst they experienced. Any unknown characteristic of a TEAE will not
be classified as worst. The level for participants who experienced no TEAE will be coded as the
zero-level of each characteristic.
```

```text
6.6.2    Safety Labs
The absolute level and the absolute change from baseline for each safety laboratory assay will be
summarized as means, standard deviations, medians, and ranges at each visit by treatment group
in all safety samples. The proportion of participants with abnormal levels classified to a toxicity
grade based on quantitative grading using National Cancer Institute (NCI) Common
Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events (CTCAE) version 5.0, the proportion with abnormal
and clinically significant safety lab levels, and the proportion with possible DILI by each
definition will be summarized by treatment group by visit and for any post-baseline visit in all
safety samples. The proportion of participants with abnormal levels by CTCAE grade, the
proportion with lab levels that are below the lower limit of normal, within normal limits, or
above the upper limit of normal will be presented as shift tables vs. the status of each participant
at baseline for each visit and for any post-baseline visit by treatment group in all safety samples.
6.6.3    ECG Results
The absolute level and the absolute change from baseline for each ECG parameter will be
summarized as means, standard deviations, medians, and ranges at each visit by treatment group
in all safety samples. The proportion of participants with abnormal and clinically significant
ECG findings or parameter values of special concern will be summarized by finding for each
treatment group by visit and at any post-baseline visit in all safety samples. The parameter values
of special concern are the following: QTcF > 480 ms, QTcF > 500 ms, increase from baseline in
QTcF > 60 ms, increase from baseline in PR > 25% to a value above 200 ms, increase from
baseline in HR > 25% to a value below 50 bpm, increase from baseline in HR > 25% to a value
above 100 bpm, and increase from baseline in QRS > 25% to a value above 110 ms.
6.6.4    Vital Signs and Weight
The absolute level and the absolute change from baseline for vital signs and weight will be
summarized as means, standard deviations, medians, and ranges at each visit by treatment group
in all safety samples. Changes in weight will also be analyzed as an efficacy endpoint by
repeated-measures and random-slopes mixed models in the FAS, ECC, ERO, ECM, and EPP
samples.
6.6.5    Suicidality
The proportion of participants who report any post-baseline suicidal ideation or any post-baseline
suicidal behavior on the C-SSRS will be summarized by treatment group in all safety samples.
Suicidal behaviors will include: actual attempts, non-suicidal self-injurious behavior, interrupted
attempts, aborted attempts, preparatory acts or behaviors, and completed suicide. The most
severe ideation, maximal frequency, maximal duration, minimal controllability, minimal
deterrents, maximal reasons for ideation, maximal actual lethality or medical damage, and
maximal potential lethality will be summarized by treatment group in all safety samples.
```

```text
6.7.5    Blindedness
The proportions of participants and site investigators who report on the Exit Questionnaire a
guess of active vs. placebo treatment assignment, each level of surety of that guess, and each of
five pre-specified reasons for making a treatment assignment will be summarized by treatment
group in the FAS, ERO, and EPP analysis sets. Treatment-dependent differences in the
proportion guessing active treatment assignment will be tested among all respondents and among
those stating they are at least somewhat sure of their guess by Fisher's exact test, the difference
in proportion guessing active treatment assignment will be estimated with confidence bounds,
and the distribution of surety of guesses will be compared by exact Cochran–Armitage trend test.
6.7.6    Protocol Deviations
The number of major and minor protocol deviations will be summarized by type of deviation and
treatment group in all analysis sets. Listings of all protocol deviations will be produced.

7. Validation

7.1     Primary Efficacy Analysis
The primary efficacy analysis will be validated according to a validation plan administered by
Berry Consultants.

7.2     Secondary, Exploratory, and Safety Analyses
Key secondary analyses of the primary efficacy endpoint and primary analyses of secondary
efficacy endpoints and summaries and analyses of serious TEAEs will be replicated from Study
Data Tabulation Model (SDTM) source data by two analysts or statisticians working
independently. Exact matches are required to validate results for the following integer results:
treatment- and visit-specific counts of efficacy endpoints, counts of events, number of
participants affected, and number of participants at risk for safety endpoints. Relative matches
within 0.1% of the common mean are required to validate results for the following floating point
results: estimates, standard errors, confidence bounds, and p-values.
All other analysis code and results will be reviewed by a statistician other than the initial analyst.
```

```text
5.0 Introduction

The ALS Master Protocol is a perpetual, multicenter, multi-regimen, randomized, placebo-
controlled, adaptive platform trial. The primary objective of the trial is to evaluate the
efficacy of multiple investigational products, as compared to placebo, on ALS disease
progression. The master protocol will include multiple regimens where a regimen is
defined as both an active treatment and the corresponding randomized placebo.
Participants in the master protocol will be randomized in two stages; first to one of the
actively enrolling regimens; and then 3:1 to active treatment or placebo within the
regimen.

This document describes the master protocol recommended primary endpoint, primary
analysis, and interim analyses. This will be the default statistical design and primary
analysis for all regimens investigated in this master protocol. The recommended design is
intended to support inference of substantial evidence of effectiveness when the data so
warrant.

Each regimen will have a regimen-specific appendix (RSA). Deviations from the master
protocol recommended statistical design are permitted. The exact statistical design along
with complete operating characteristics for each regimen will be specified in the RSA.

The master protocol recommended primary efficacy endpoint is change from baseline
through week 24 in disease severity as measured by the ALS Functional Rating Scale-
Revised (ALSFRS-R) total score and survival. The primary analysis is a Bayesian shared
parameter analysis of ALSFRS-R and survival. The primary analysis compares each
regimen-specific active treatment to a shared control group.
```

```text
Cudkowicz (PI) HEALEY ALS Platform Trial: Master Statistical Analysis Plan   Version 3.0, 06 Feb 2023


The sample size enrolled in each regimen will be determined based on the specifics of the
intervention, anticipated effect size, and number of treatment arms evaluated within that
regimen. There are frequent interim analyses in order to potentially stop a regimen early
for success or for futility. The default design for a regimen will include stopping early only
for futility. Interim analyses will be conducted at the same time for all regimens within the
master protocol, every 12 weeks of trial time. A regimen is eligible for these interim
analyses once a minimum specified amount of data has been observed for that regimen.


2.0 Primary Efficacy Analysis

The following describes the Bayesian shared parameter primary analysis method of
function and survival for each regimen, the primary analysis population, prior distributions
for all model parameters, and specific posterior summaries of interest that will be used in
the design. We denote the regimen being analyzed as the “analysis regimen” throughout.

2.1 Primary Analysis Population and Shared Control

The primary analysis population for each analysis regimen is guided by the intent to treat
(ITT) principle and includes all participants randomized to the active treatment arm within
the analysis regimen, all participants concurrently randomized to the control arm within
the analysis regimen, and control participants from other regimens from a shared control,
excluding any participant determined to not meet ALS diagnostic criteria.
The primary analysis dataset uses longitudinal data on disease progression (ALSFRS-R
measurements at 0, 4, 8, 12, 16, 20 and 24 weeks, at early termination visits, and
assessments during double-blind follow-up that substitute for missed scheduled visits) for
those who have not been censored due to reaching the survival endpoint (a composite of
death or death-equivalent) as well as occurrence of the survival endpoint during planned
double-blind follow-up on participants within the primary analysis population. Follow-up
for primary analysis of survival among participants who do not die or reach a death
equivalent prior to the Week 24 Visit will be censored at the Week 24 Visit date, if
completed, or at the earlier of the end of the Week 24 Visit window or date last known alive
if a Week 24 visit is not completed. Off-schedule results, e.g., those collected as part of an
Early Termination Visit, will be used in place of the closest missing scheduled visit (while
preserving visit sequence) for analyses that depend on visit-specific data. Off-schedule
results that do not substitute for a missed schedule visit will not be used.

The shared control group will include participants randomized to the control arm across
other actively enrolling regimens (concurrent shared controls) and previously completed
regimens (non-concurrent shared controls) within the master protocol as specified in the
RSA. Concurrent shared controls are defined as participants on a control arm who were
randomized within a 6-month window of the start and stop of randomization of the
analysis regimen. For the purposes of analysis, 6 months is defined as 180 days. For
example, if the analysis regimen enrolls from Month 12 – Month 24 in the platform, the
```

```text
Cudkowicz (PI) HEALEY ALS Platform Trial: Master Statistical Analysis Plan   Version 3.0, 06 Feb 2023


concurrent shared controls will include all available data from all participants randomized

For interim analyses, all available data at the time of the analysis will be used including
data on participants who have not had the opportunity to complete 24 weeks of follow-up
(denoted ongoing). The phrase “opportunity to complete 24 weeks” is meant to describe
participants who have at least 24 weeks of time elapse since their baseline visit.
For final primary analyses, data from ongoing participants will not be used, but
participants from open regimens who have had the opportunity to complete 24 weeks of
follow-up may be used. To minimize risk of potential unblinding of data for regimens
outside of the analysis regimen that have not yet had last patient last visit (open regimens)
if shared control data from open regimens are used within the primary analysis, no
participant-level data will be shared and no summaries of the data will be provided from
which individual participants could be identified until all regimens that are included in the
shared control for the analysis regimen have been closed (last patient last visit) and locked.
For this reason, regimens may choose to use shared control data only from regimens that
have been closed (last patient last visit) to avoid delay in release of participant-level data.
The shared control population that will be included in the final primary analysis for each
regimen will be prespecified in each regimen-specific appendix.
Finally, the master protocol allows for additional and more restrictive inclusion/exclusion
criteria within a regimen specific appendix (RSA). However, these additional
inclusion/exclusion criteria will be limited and will be allowed based only on restrictions
due to safety or mechanism of action. Major additional RSA inclusion/exclusion criteria
that can be assessed for all participants entering the platform will be used to subset the
shared control to include only shared control participants that meet these major additional
RSA inclusion/exclusion criteria. Major additional RSA inclusion/exclusion criteria that
will be used to subset the shared control will be limited to those criteria that have the
potential to lead to significant differences in the patient population in terms of the rate of
disease progression within the primary analysis population and will be prespecified within
each RSA. The first three regimens enrolling within the platform did not include any major
additional inclusion/exclusion criteria.



2.2 Bayesian Shared Parameter Model of Function and Mortality

The primary analysis for each analysis regimen is a Bayesian shared parameter model of
function and survival with the following components:

           x   Function: Bayesian repeated measures model of ALSFRS-R at baseline
               through 24 weeks for all participants who have not experienced mortality
               (per definition of the composite survival endpoint). Participants who have
               experienced mortality will not contribute to the functional component of the
               analysis. The Bayesian repeated measures model of ALSFRS-R measures the
```

```text
Cudkowicz (PI) HEALEY ALS Platform Trial: Master Statistical Analysis Plan         Version 3.0, 06 Feb 2023


                slowing in the rate of progression for ALSFRS-R in treated participants
                relative to control for survivors.
            x Mortality: Exponential proportional hazards model of time to the composite
                survival endpoint of death or death-equivalent through 24 weeks that
                measures the mortality hazard ratio in treated participants relative to
                control.
            x Shared Treatment Effect: A shared treatment effect parameter (slowing in
                time of the progression of the disease) is common between the function and
                mortality components and provides an overall estimate of the treatment
                effect that is averaged across the two endpoints. This shared parameter
                corresponds to a slowing in the rate of ALSFRS-R progression as well as a
                slowing in the time to mortality.
The ALSFRS-R repeated measures component of survivors is based on a linear rate of
progression in ALSFRS-R for placebo participants and a proportional slowing in the rate of
progression for treated participants at each time point. The model incorporates
participant-level random effects in the baseline value of the ALSFRS-R (intercept) and in
the rate of progression (slope); covariate effects to account for covariate-explainable
differences in rates of progression based on participant-specific baseline covariates;
regimen-specific differences in baseline values, rates of progression and measurement
error, and a time-trend effect to accommodate non-concurrently randomized controls.

the value for j will be the actual week that the visit took place rounded to the nearest day.

The mortality component is an exponential proportional hazards model that assumes a
constant baseline hazard rate for all control participants. Given the low expected rate of
mortality within this study (5%) and to avoid over-parameterization, we do not include
covariate, regimen-specific, or time-trend effects in the mortality component. Assuming
there are ܰ௠௢௥௧ total participants, the hazard rate of mortality per month for participants
݅ = 1, … , ܰ௠௢௥௧ is denoted ߣ௜ and is
```

```text
Each parameter in the above model and the prior distribution assumed for each parameter
is described below in Sections 2.2.1 – 2.2.7. The majority of the prior distributions are
chosen to be non-informative (large amount of variability and uncertainty) to allow the
posterior distributions to reflect what is observed in the data.

2.2.1 Treatment Effect ALSFRS-R and Mortality

The disease rate ratio (DRR) under treatment T is the parameter exp(ߠ் ). For a placebo
patient, the DRR is set equal to 1 (ߠ଴ = 0). Thus, the DRR parameter for active treatment
represents the relative change to the rate of decline of ALSFRS-R and the rate of mortality
of treated participant relative to a placebo participant. The estimated DRR can also be
interpreted as the average rate of decline in function and mortality, with the weights across
function and mortality depending on the proportion of patients with observed mortality.
Appropriately, more weight is given to mortality relative to function as the proportion of
observed mortality increases. If the DRR is less than 1 then the rate of disease progression
on function and mortality for actively treated participants is slower than that for placebo.
The DRR value represents the proportional slowing in time (or increase) of the disease. A
DRR = 0.75 corresponds to a 25% slowing in the rate of functional progression and time to
mortality and a value of 0.25 represents a 75% slowing in the rate of functional
progression and time to mortality.

The prior distribution for the DRR for the regimen-specific active treatment, T=1, is
uniform between 0 and 2; placing equal weight on a DRR<1 and DRR>1:

                                       exp(ߠଵ ) ~ܷ݂݊݅(0,2).


2.2.2 Random Intercept and Slope Effects for ALSFRS-R


Participant-specific random intercept effects, ߛ௜ , ݅ = 1, … , ܰ, represent the increase or
decrease in the participant-specific expected ALSFRS-R at the time of randomization
compared to the overall mean for the treatment arm and regimen. Participant-specific
```

```text
random slope effects, ‫ܫ‬, ݅ = 1, … , ܰ, represent the participant-specific rate of decline per
month in ALSFRS-R expected under placebo.

The overall mean estimate per treatment arm and regimen is assumed to have a non-
informative prior normal distribution with a mean of 38 (mean value observed in historical
trials within the PRO-ACT database) and SD of 10:

                             ߙோ,் ~ܰ(38, 10ଶ ); ܶ = 0,1; ܴ = 1, … ܰோ

Participant-level random intercept effects are modelled using a hierarchical normal
distribution with a population mean of 0 and population standard deviation, ߪఊ , with a
uniform hyper-prior:

                                     ߛ௜ ~ܰ൫0, ߪఊଶ ൯; ݅ = 1, … ܰ;
                                         ߪఊ ~ܷ݂݊݅(0,10).

The uniform hyper-prior on the standard deviation is non-informative with a range from 0
to 10 (twice observed SD of the participant-level intercepts in historical trials within the
PRO-ACT database).

Participant-level random slope effects are modelled using a hierarchical normal
distribution with overall population mean, ߤఉ , with a normal hyper-prior; and an arm-
specific population standard deviation, ߪఉ,் ; ܶ = 0,1, with a uniform hyper-prior:

                                                ଶ
                                   ߚ௜ ~ܰ൫ߤఉ , ߪఉ,௧(௜) ൯; ݅ = 1, … ܰ;
                                            ߤఉ ~ܰ(1,1ଶ );
                                      ߪఉ,் ~ܷ݂݊݅(0,2); ܶ = 0,1.

The mean and variance of the participant-level random effects are estimated from the data.
This is done by placing hyperprior distributions on the relevant parameters in the random
effects distribution and using the observed data to estimate the hyper-parameters. Non-
informative distributions are used for these hyper-priors to ensure that the data determine
the corresponding hyper-prior estimates and the estimated random effect distributions. In
particular, the normal hyper-prior on the mean of the participant-level random slope
effects is non-informative with a mean of 1 (mean value observed in historical trials within
the PRO-ACT database) with a standard deviation of 1. The uniform hyper-prior on the
standard deviation is non-informative with a range from 0 to 2 (over twice observed SD of
the participant-level slopes in historical trials within the PRO-ACT database).
```

```text
2.2.4 Time-Trend Effects ALSFRS-R

The log of the time-trend effects will be modelled using a simple normal dynamic linear
model where the distribution of each time-trend effect is normal with a mean equal to the
effect at the previous time period and a standard deviation that has a uniform hyper-prior:

2.2.5 Baseline Covariate Effects ALSFRS-R

Covariates included in the primary efficacy analysis are: time since onset, pre-baseline
slope of ALSFRS-R, baseline log-transformed serum NfL level, baseline riluzole use,
baseline edaravone use, and baseline sodium phenylbutyrate/taurursodiol use as indicated
in participants’ concomitant medication logs. All covariates will be standardized by
subtracting their means using all participants in the regimen-specific analysis data set
(regimen-specific treatment and control as well as additional shared control). Missing
baseline covariates for a participant will be imputed using the mean value across all
participants in the applicable analysis dataset (regimen-specific treatment and control as
well as additional shared control for the primary analysis). For any baseline covariate that
is transformed for analysis, means will be calculated after transformation. Covariate effects
are modelled as exp(ߜ௖ ), ܿ = 1, … , ‫ܥ‬, accounting for covariate-explainable variability in the
rate of ALSFRS-R progression due to participant-specific baseline covariates.

Prior distributions for the log of the covariate effects are independent and identically
distributed as:

                                     ߜ௖ ~ܰ(0, 1ଶ ); ܿ = 1, … ‫ܥ‬.
```

```text
the value observed within the PRO-ACT database.

2.3 Posterior Summaries

The Bayesian posterior distribution of all model parameters is calculated using Markov
chain Monte Carlo (MCMC). We will draw at least 500,000 samples from the posterior
distribution with a thinning of 10. The exact number of samples from the MCMC will be
determined based on convergence diagnostics to ensure an adequate effective sample size
of the posterior distribution.

Summaries of the Bayesian posterior distribution for the DRR under the regimen-specific
active treatment, exp(ߠଵ ), will be provided including the mean and 95% credible intervals;
the probability that the treatment is superior to placebo, ܲ‫( ݎ‬exp(ߠଵ ) < 1); and the
probability the treatment slows the rate of progression by at least 10%, ܲ‫( ݎ‬exp(ߠଵ ) < .9).


3.0 Success Thresholds, Interim Analyses and Trial Adaptations

Interim analyses will occur simultaneously for all actively enrolling regimens. Interims will
begin after at least one regimen within the master protocol has 40 randomized participants
with the opportunity to complete at least 24 weeks of follow-up. Subsequent interims will
occur approximately every 12 weeks. At an interim analysis, a regimen can either stop
```

```text
Cudkowicz (PI) HEALEY ALS Platform Trial: Master Statistical Analysis Plan   Version 3.0, 06 Feb 2023


early for success or for futility. A regimen is eligible to stop early for success or for futility
once there are 40 randomized participants within that regimen with the opportunity to
complete at least 24 weeks of follow-up. If a regimen has not stopped early for success or
futility at an interim analysis, the regimen-specific final analysis will take place after all
participants randomized to that regimen (typically 120 active and 40 control) have had the
opportunity to complete 24 weeks of follow-up.

3.1 Regimen Success

In the default design, early success stopping at interim analyses will not be conducted.
Success will be declared for a regimen at the final analysis if the posterior probability that
the treatment, T=1, is superior to placebo group is greater than .979:

                                    ܲ‫( ݎ‬exp(ߠଵ ) < 1) > .979.

The threshold for the final analysis of .979 was chosen to control type I error at 2.5%
across all Null simulation scenarios (except for when the analysis regimen has a slower
rate of progression on ALSFRS-R compared to all other regimens) without futility stopping.



3.2 Regimen Futility

Futility will be declared for a regimen at an interim analysis if the posterior probability that
the treatment, T=1, slows disease progression by at least 10% is less than 5%:
```

```text
    Mortality

5.0     As a key supportive analysis, a joint rank test of survival and function will be
    performed. The analysis will rank all participants based first on survival time and
    second, if they survived, the change in ALSFRS-R from 0 to 24-weeks as described in
    Berry et al. 2013 (Berry, 2013). Given that participants may have different lengths of
    follow-up, comparisons will be made between any two participants based on the
    shortest duration of follow-up between the two. For example, if participant 1 has not
    experienced mortality and has ALSFRS-R values through 12 weeks and participant 2
    has not experienced mortality and has ALSFRS-R values through 24 weeks, the
    comparison of the change from baseline at 12 weeks in ALSFRS-R will be made.
    Additionally, if participant 1 has ALSFRS-R values through 36 weeks and then
    experienced mortality at 36 weeks and participant 2 has ALSFRS-R values only through
    24 weeks, a comparison is made based on ALSFRS-R values through 24 weeks. A linear
    regression analysis will be performed on the ranks to adjust for important covariates
    described in Section 2.2.5 using a fixed effect for each covariate as well as a treatment
    effect. This secondary analysis will be performed on the primary analysis population as
    well as the sensitivity analysis populations described in Section 4.3.3 Sensitivity of
    Analysis Population

As sensitivity analyses, the Bayesian primary analysis model will be applied to the
following analysis populations:

   x   Concurrent Shared Control Population: All participants randomized to the active
       treatment arm or placebo arm within the analysis regimen and all participants
       randomized to the placebo arm across other regimens within the master protocol
       who have been randomized within a 6-month window of when the analysis regimen
       has been enrolling.
   x   Common Mode of Administration Population: All participants randomized to the
       active treatment arm or placebo arm within the analysis regimen and all
       participants randomized to the placebo arm across other regimens within the
       master protocol that have the same mode of administration.
```

```text
Cudkowicz (PI) HEALEY ALS Platform Trial: Master Statistical Analysis Plan   Version 3.0, 06 Feb 2023



5.0 Clinical Trial Simulations

Clinical trial simulation is used to provided simulated example trials and to quantify
operating characteristics for each regimen. In particular, virtual patient outcomes are
created under different assumptions for key design parameters including placebo rates of
progression for ALSFRS-R, measurement error for ALSFRS-R, mortality rates, treatment
effects on ALSFRS-R, treatment effects on mortality, dropout rates and accrual rates. For
each set of simulation assumptions (i.e., a scenario), many trials are simulated and virtually
executed, including all interim analyses for early success and early futility. Trial operating
characteristics are summarized across all simulated trials for each scenario.


5.1 Virtual Patient ALSFRS-R Simulation

Participant-level ALSFRS-R data at each timepoint is simulated using placebo data from the
Pooled Resource Open-Access ALS Clinical Trials Database (PRO-ACT) with a bootstrap
approach. In particular, a simulation database is created from the PRO-ACT database using
each placebo participant with at least 3 ALSFRS-R measurements over the course of at least
24 weeks. For each of these participants, an intercept, slope and residual standard-error is
calculated from a participant-specific linear regression of the measured ALSFRS-R within
the PRO-ACT database as a function of the month of each measured visit. These linear
regression values as well as baseline covariates are recorded for each participant within
the simulation database.

Within the current simulations, a subset of the PRO-ACT simulation database is sampled
based on those participants that have a time since onset less than 3 years and have a slow
vital capacity or forced vital capacity (SVC / FVC) greater than 50% (N=2175). Within this
subset, the average subject-specific slope (ALSFRS-R decline per month) is -1.03 with a SD
of 0.76. The average residual standard error is 1.77.
To create a dataset of participant-level ALSFRS-R scores at each 4-week visit for each
simulated clinical trial from the PRO-ACT simulation database, the following steps are
taken:

   x   Choose a participant, i, from the PRO-ACT simulation database and record values of
       the participant-specific intercept (ߛ௜ ), slope (ߚ௜ ), residual standard error (ߪ௜ଶ ), and
       baseline covariates.
   x   Simulate participant-specific ALSFRS-R at 0, 4, 8, 12, 16, 20 and 24 weeks (j) from:
                                                               ௝‫כ‬ଵଶ
                                  ܻ௜,௝ ~ܰ(ߛ௜ + (ߚ௜ + ߠ௧(௜) ) ‫ כ‬ହଶ , ߪ௜ଶ );
       assuming ߠ଴ = 0 for placebo and ߠଵ is found such that (ߚመ + ߠଵ )/ߚመ = 1- assumed %
       slowing of treatment; where ߚመ is the average slope across all subjects.

The treatment effect within the simulations is introduced as an additive effect to avoid
introducing differences in the variance of the participant-specific rates of progression
between the treatment and control arms. This is to provide a conservative estimate of
```

```text
5.2 Base Simulation Scenarios and Sensitivity Scenarios

All trial simulations assume there are 3 regimens within the platform; all are enrolling
within the same population and randomizing concurrently (as defined in Section 2.1).
Treatment effect assumptions will vary across the first regimen and operating
characteristics will be reported for this regimen. The other two regimens will be assumed
to be Null (no benefit on ALSFRS-R or mortality).

The trial simulations are performed under a base scenario (set of simulation assumptions)
as well as numerous sensitivity scenarios. In particular, a simulation scenario is defined by
6 different parameters (Table 5.2.1). This set of 6 parameters characterizes various
nuisance parameters, but also key assumptions on the endpoints such as their variability
and the behavior of the control arm and treatment effect. We define a “base case” set of
values for these 6 parameters as well as two sensitivity analysis values. The table below
lists each of these 6 parameters along with the base case value and the two sensitivity
analysis values.
Table 5.2.2 describes treatment effect assumptions. We simulate the base case of
parameter values from Table 5.2.1 under all treatment effect scenarios in Table 5.2.2 and
consider this as the primary set of operating characteristics for this trial (6 scenarios).
Then, for each of 6 parameters that define a scenario, we vary one sensitivity analysis value
at a time from the base case assumptions. For the first 5 parameter values we simulate
under the null treatment effect and the common treatment effect with 30% slowing and a
HR of .7 (10 sensitivity analyses across 2 treatment effect assumptions). For parameter 6
(mortality rate) we simulate under 4 treatment effect scenarios (Null, Common Effect with
HR=.7 and Slowing=30%, No Mortality Benefit, and Worse Mortality). All sensitivity
scenarios will be simulated with and without futility stopping.
```

```text
Cudkowicz (PI) HEALEY ALS Platform Trial: Master Statistical Analysis Plan   Version 3.0, 06 Feb 2023



6.0 Example Trials and Operating Characteristics

For each scenario and treatment effect we simulate 10000 clinical trials. We present
example simulated trials as well as overall average operating characteristics across all
simulated trials. For each simulation scenario and treatment effect, we report the following
regimen-specific operating characteristics for the first regimen:

     x   Mean duration (months)
     x   Mean sample size randomized to the regimen
     x   Probability of early success, total probability of success, and cumulative probability
         of stopping for success as a function of trial duration.
     x   Probability of early futility and cumulative probability of stopping for futility as a
         function of trial duration.
     x   Average estimate of the treatment effect and estimate of the treatment effect as a
         function of total trial duration.

6.1 Operating Characteristics Base Scenarios

Table 6.1.1 shows the overall operating characteristics for a single regimen under the
master protocol recommended design for the Base simulation scenarios with futility
stopping. Additionally, Table 6.1.2 summarizes the observed treatment effect estimates in
each simulated trial as a function of the trial duration and outcome.

Under the null scenario there is an overall one-sided Type I error rate of 2.4% for the
regimen. The average duration of the regimen is 14 months. There is a 28.0% chance that
the regimen will be stopped early for futility.

The master protocol recommended design has 61%, 77%, and 88% power to detect a
slowing in the rate of disease progression of 25%, 30% and 35% respectively when
mortality and function have a common treatment effect. When there is no benefit in
mortality and a 30% slowing in ALSFRS-R progression the power drops from 77% to 72%.
When there is a negative effect on mortality and a 30% slowing in ALSFRS-R progression,
the power drops further to 68%. Finally, there is less than 1% chance in these scenarios
that the regimen will be stopped early for futility.

Given the expected accrual rate, interim analyses will begin approximately 7 months into
the trial. Therefore, the final analysis for a regimen is expected to occur around 16 months
after that regimen is initiated. Table 6.1.2 summarizes the observed treatment effect
estimates that would lead to early stopping for futility or final success. If a regimen stops at
the first interim analysis for futility, the average treatment effect estimate (DRR) is 1.35.
The smallest observed DRR stopping for futility at this look across all simulated trials was
1.13, corresponding to a 13-35% increase in the rate of disease progression for treatment
relative to placebo. At later analyses, 10-13 months from the start of the regimen, the
average treatment effect estimate (DRR) observed among regimens that stopped for futility
was 1.16-1.21 and the smallest observed DRR across all simulated trials observed to stop
```

```text
6.2 Operating Characteristics and Example Trials Integration of Mortality and
    Function

This section provides sensitivity of the overall operating characteristics to the assumptions
of an overall 5% 6-month mortality rate as well as the assumption that mortality and
function have a common treatment effect. All operating characteristics in this section
assume no futility stopping. Additionally, we show results from two example trials to
demonstrate how the proposed Bayesian shared parameter primary analysis accounts for
mortality.
Table 6.2.1 provides Type I error, power and DRR estimates for the base scenario (5% 6-
month mortality) as well as scenarios when the mortality rate is increased (10-20% 6-
month mortality). Power and average DRR estimates are provided for treatment effect
scenarios where the true DRR for mortality and function is the same and equal to .70 and
when the true DRR for mortality and function is different (null or negative effect on
mortality).
Under the null scenario when the true DRR equals 1.0 for function and true HR=1.0 for
mortality, the type I error and the estimated DRR are not sensitive to the assumption of the
percentage of 6-month mortality.
Under scenarios when the treatment effect for mortality is null or worse (true HR=1.0 or
HR=1.3) and there is a 30% slowing in ALSFRS-R (true DRR=0.70), the power and
estimated DRR decrease as the rate of mortality increases. This is due to the overall
treatment effect being more heavily weighted by the mortality effect. The average
estimated DRR equals 0.69 when the true DRR and HR parameters equal 0.70. Under a
20% 6-month mortality rate and a true DRR equal to 0.70, the average estimated DRR
equals 0.75 and 0.81 when the true mortality HR equals 1.0 and 1.3, respectively, thereby
decreasing the estimated functional treatment effect by 6-12% (absolute) compared to the
scenario with both true DRR and true HR equal 0.70.
```

```text
Cudkowicz (PI) HEALEY ALS Platform Trial: Master Statistical Analysis Plan   Version 3.0, 06 Feb 2023


Additionally, to demonstrate that our model properly accounts for mortality and to show
the sensitivity of the model to treatment-dependent differences in mortality, two simulated
trials have been explored (Tables 6.2.2 and 6.2.3 and Figures 6.2.1-6.2.2). Within each
simulated trial, we fix the ALSFRS-R data for each participant as well as the mortality data
of participants in the placebo arm. We then let the mortality data on the active treatment
arm vary to explore how the key supportive joint rank analysis (CAFS) and the proposed
Bayesian shared-parameter primary analysis respond to an increasing number of deaths
on the active treatment arm. The point at which inference about benefit from treatment
switches from significant to non-significant, the tipping point, reveals how sensitive a given
analysis is to an excess of deaths in the active arm. An analysis with a smaller tipping point
is more sensitive and would be less likely to support a claim of benefit from the active
treatment when the active treatment slows functional progression does not also reduce
mortality rates.
The base simulations assume a substantial slowing in rate of functional decline of 30%.
Within these example trials, as more participants on the active treatment arm experience
mortality, the estimated disease rate ratio (DRR), the common treatment effect estimate
between mortality and function, increases towards 1 (no effect of treatment) and the
posterior probability that the treatment slows the rate of progression (DRR < 1) decreases.
This is similar to the change seen in the CAFS joint rank analysis: as mortality in the active
treatment arm increases, its p-value becomes less significative (see details below). The two
example trials differ in the overall mortality rate (Example 1 has a higher rate than
Example 2). Comparison of the two example trials shows that the sensitivity of the results
to the mortality hazard ratio depends on the overall mortality rate (the mortality hazard
ratio is smaller at the tipping point for Example 1 than for Example 2). These examples
demonstrate that the proposed Bayesian shared-parameter model is more sensitive to
discrepancies in the effect of treatment on mortality vs. the effect of treatment on function
relative to the CAFS joint rank analysis.

For each example, we tabulate the following parameters across a range of mortality rates in
the active treatment arm (Tables 6.2.2 and 6.2.3): the number of deaths on active treatment
and shared placebo, the posterior DRR parameter estimates from the shared-parameter
analysis vs. the sensitivity analysis that allows different DRR treatment effects between
function and mortality, CAFS one-sided p-value, and posterior probability that the DRR is
less than 1 from the shared parameter model. For Example 1, we present several graphs
from specific representative trial simulations (the highlighted rows from Table 6.2.2; Plots
6.2.1-6.2.2). The top left plot shows estimates of change from baseline in ALSFRS-R through
24-weeks. The results include fitted values for the active treatment and the shared placebo
from the Bayesian shared-parameter model (solid lines), fitted values for the active
treatment and the shared placebo from the sensitivity analysis with different treatment
effects on mortality and function (dotted lines), and raw mean values of the change from
baseline at each visit (circles). The top right plot is a residual plot of fitted ALSFRS-R value
(x-axis) at each visit vs. the residual ALSFRS-R value (y-axis) from the Bayesian shared-
parameter model. The color of the circle represents active treatment vs. shared placebo.
The size of the circle represents visit time (24-week visits are larger circles than 4-week
visits). The bottom left plot shows Kaplan-Meier survival curves for mortality for the active
```

```text
Cudkowicz (PI) HEALEY ALS Platform Trial: Master Statistical Analysis Plan   Version 3.0, 06 Feb 2023


treatment and the shared placebo as well as the fitted exponential curves from the
Bayesian shared-parameter model. The bottom right plot shows the DRR parameter
estimates from the shared-parameter analysis vs. the sensitivity analysis that allows
different DRR treatment effects between function and mortality.
Within Example Trial 1 (Table 6.2.2 and Figures 6.2.1-6.2.2), there are 18 deaths on the
placebo arm (15%). Results are shown for 11-29 total deaths on the treatment arm with
mortality hazard ratios from the mortality component alone ranging from 0.68 to 1.58.
Simulations representative of rows highlighted in Table 6.2.2 are presented in Figures
6.2.1-6.2.2. The treatment effect estimated from the functional component alone for those
participants who did not experience mortality range from 0.71-0.73. As the number of
deaths increases on the active treatment arm, the posterior estimate for the shared
treatment effect between mortality and function from the Bayesian shared-parameter
model increases (0.73 to 0.88) and the posterior probability that the DRR is less than one
decreases (0.994 to 0.853). Similarly, the CAFS one-sided p-value increases (0.002 to
0.233). For all mortality hazard ratios less than or equal to one, both CAFS and the Bayesian
shared-parameter model indicate a significant treatment benefit. For the Bayesian shared-
parameter model, the tipping point at which results become non-significant occurs
between mortality hazard ratios of 1.04 and 1.10 (17 to 18 deaths in the active treatment
arm). Under the CAFS analysis, the tipping point at which results become non-significant
occurs between mortality hazard ratios of 1.15 to 1.27 (19 to 21 deaths on the active
treatment arm). Under the simulated conditions, the Bayesian shared-parameter model
was more sensitive to a discrepancy between the effect of treatment on function and
mortality than the CAFS analysis.
Within Example Trial 2 (Table 6.2.3), there are 11 deaths on the placebo arm (9%). Results
are shown for 6-19 total deaths on the treatment arm with mortality hazard ratios from the
mortality component alone ranging from 0.66 to 1.57. The treatment effect estimated from
the functional component alone for those participants who did not experience mortality
ranges from 0.72-0.75. As the number of deaths increases on the active treatment arm, the
posterior estimate for the shared treatment effect between mortality and function from the
Bayesian shared-parameter model increases (0.71 to 0.85) and the posterior probability
that the DRR is less than one decreases (0.998 to 0.917). Similarly, the CAFS one-sided p-
value increases (0.001 to 0.085). For all mortality hazard ratios less than or equal to 1.13
both CAFS and the Bayesian shared-parameter model indicate a significant treatment
benefit (CAFS p-value less than .025 and Bayesian posterior greater than 0.979). For the
Bayesian shared-parameter model, the tipping point at which results become non-
significant occurs between mortality hazard ratios of 1.13 to 1.21 (11 to 12 deaths on the
active treatment arm). Under the CAFS analysis, the tipping point at which results become
non-significant occurs between mortality hazard ratios of 1.27 to 1.33 (13 to 14 deaths on
the active treatment arm). As in Example Trial 1, the Bayesian shared-parameter model
was more sensitive to a discrepancy between the effect of treatment on function and
mortality than the CAFS analysis. We also note that with the lower mortality rate, both the
Bayesian shared-parameter analysis and the CAFS analysis were less sensitive to a
discrepancy between the effect of treatment on function and mortality.
```

```text
6.3 Operating Characteristics and Example Trials Differences Across Regimens

This section provides sensitivity of the overall operating characteristics to the assumption
that regimens within the shared control have the same ALSFRS-R distributions. In
particular, we look at the sensitivity of operating characteristics to differences across the
regimens in the rate of progression of ALSFRS-R as well as differences in the residual error
of ALSFRS-R. All operating characteristics in this section assume no futility stopping.
Table 6.3 provides Type I error, power and DRR estimates for the base scenario (all
regimens have the same ALSFRS-R progression rate and residual error) as well as
scenarios when the analysis regimen differs from all other regimens in terms of the
ALSFRS-R progression rate and ALSFRS-R residual error. Power and DRR estimates are
provided for treatment effect scenarios where the DRR for mortality and function is the
same and equal to .70 and when the DRR for mortality and function is different (null or
negative effect on mortality).

Under the extreme assumption that participants in the analysis regimen progress at a 10%
slower rate of progression on ALSFRS-R than those in all other regimens, there is an
increased Type I error of 6% and a small positive bias in the treatment effect estimate of
.96 (4% slowing in progression). Under the assumption that participants in the analysis
regimen progress at a 10% faster rate of progression on ALSFRS-R than those in all other
regimens, the Type I error decreases to 1% with a small negative bias in the treatment
effect estimate equal to 1.05 (5% increase in progression). The sensitivity of these results is
due to the borrowing across regimens for the shared control.

Operating characteristics are not sensitive to differences in the ALSFRS-R residual error for
the analysis regimen compared to other regimens other than having an increase or
decrease in power when ALSFRS-R under the analysis regimen has more or less residual
error.
In addition to the primary analysis, sensitivity analyses will be provided with regards to the
shared control (Section 4.2) to assess differences in rates of progression across regimens
and how treatment effect estimates differ under the analysis regimen when the treated
```

```text
Cudkowicz (PI) HEALEY ALS Platform Trial: Master Statistical Analysis Plan   Version 3.0, 06 Feb 2023


group is compared to only the regimen-specific controls and compared to only the controls
with the same mode of administration. We provide examples from simulated trials
(Figures 6.3.1-6.3.3) to demonstrate how discrepancies between the primary analysis and
proposed sensitivity analyses will address the concern of a potential type I error and bias
due to sharing of controls that are heterogenous.

For each simulated example trial, the left plots show results from the primary shared-
parameter model for the change from baseline in ALSFRS-R through 24 weeks using all
shared controls (top) and for the sensitivity analysis using only regimen-specific controls
(bottom). The results include fitted values for the active treatment and for each regimen-
specific control (blue line for active treatment; red line for matching control from analysis
regimen; pink for additional shared controls when included in the analysis) and raw mean
values in the change from baseline at each visit (circles). The middle plots show mortality
KM plots as well as exponential fitted curves for the primary analysis using all shared
controls (top) vs. the sensitivity analysis with only regimen-specific controls (bottom). The
right plots show the DRR parameter estimates, i.e., the posterior probability of slowing
progression (DRR<1), from the primary analysis method using all shared controls (top) vs.
the sensitivity analysis using only regimen-specific controls (bottom). Additionally, we
note the one-sided p-value from the key supportive CAFS analysis using all shared controls
(top right) compared to only regimen-specific controls (bottom right).
Figure 6.3.1 shows results from a null simulated trial (no treatment effect) where there are
differences across regimens in the shared control. In particular, the matched regimen-
specific control from the analysis regimen has a slower rate of progression on ALSFRS-R
(red circles) than the controls from the other regimens in the shared control (pink circles).
The raw mean change from baseline at 24 weeks in the matched regimen-specific controls
is approximately -4.5 and the mean change from baseline at 24 weeks in the other shared
controls is approximately -6.5 and -7.2. These differences across regimens within the
shared control lead to a fitted ALSFRS-R slope for the matched regimen-specific control to
be pulled down and estimated to be larger (faster progression) than what is observed in
that regimen only (red line falls below red circles). This causes a positive treatment effect
bias in the DRR (estimated to be 0.73) under the primary analysis method. The posterior
probability that the DRR is less than 1 is 0.990 and the corresponding one-sided p-value
from the CAFS is 0.0063. Both analysis methods using shared controls would lead to a type
I error. In comparison, the sensitivity analysis using only matched regimen-specific
controls leads to a fitted value for the regimen-only controls ALSFRS-R slope that estimates
slower decline than what was estimated using all shared controls. This results in a smaller
treatment effect estimate of 0.91 under the primary shared-parameter analysis. The
posterior probability that the DRR is less than 1 is 0.683 and the corresponding one-sided
p-value from the CAFS is 0.5525. The clear discrepancies between the primary analysis
and the proposed sensitivity analysis using only regimen-specific controls in the Bayesian
shared parameter analysis as well as the CAFS analysis demonstrates that the positive
result in the primary analysis may be a type I error due to the sharing of heterogenous
controls.
```

```text
the controls are likely very different across regimens and leads to a fitted ALSFRS-R slope
for the matched regimen-specific control that is less influenced and pulled down by the
controls of the other two regimens than in the previous example (red line falls slightly
below red circles). This causes a slight positive treatment effect bias in the DRR (estimated
to be 0.90) under the primary analysis method. The posterior probability that the DRR is
less than 1 is 0.714 and the Bayesian shared parameter analysis leads to a null result.
However, because the CAFS analysis uses all shared controls and the amount of sharing is
not dynamic, the results of this analysis are more sensitive to the increased rate of

the CAFS is 0.0026. Leading to a Type I error in the CAFS analysis but not the Bayesian
```

```text
only matched regimen-specific controls leads to a similar treatment effect estimate of 0.73,
but with more uncertainty. The posterior probability that the DRR is less than 1 is 0.931
and the corresponding one-sided p-value from the CAFS is 0.1102. The consistency in
results between the primary analysis using all shared controls as well as the sensitivity
analysis using only matched regimen-specific controls provides confidence that the
positive primary analysis results are not biased by the shared controls.




Figure 6.3.3: Example Trial 5. Positive Treatment Effect (DRR = 0.70). No differences across regimens in shared control. Top
  row shows results from the primary analysis using all shared controls. Bottom row shows results from sensitivity analyses
                                        using only matched regimen-specific controls.
```

```text
Cudkowicz (PI) HEALEY ALS Platform Trial: Master Statistical Analysis Plan      Version 3.0, 06 Feb 2023


6.4 Operating Characteristics Additional Sensitivity Scenarios

This section provides additional sensitivity results of the overall operating characteristics
to the assumptions of accrual rates, dropout rates, timing of enrolling additional regimens,
and distributional assumptions of ALSFRS-R common to all regimens. All operating
characteristics in this section assume no futility stopping.

Table 6.4 shows the overall power (under a DRR of .70 for Mortality and Function) and
Type I error for the base scenarios as well as all additional sensitivity scenarios described
in Section 5.2 without futility stopping.

Under all Null scenarios, Type I error is controlled at 2.5% or less without futility stopping.
Type I error decreases as there is less shared controls in the final analysis (faster accrual
and regimens enrolling further apart).

Under all scenarios where the treatment effect is common between ALSFRS-R slowing and
mortality (DRR is 0.70) the power of the trial ranges between 56-79%. Power is the lowest
when the regimens start six months apart since the first regimen will have less shared
control. Mean DRR estimates range from 0.69 – 0.71 (29-31% slowing).

    Table 6.4: Base and Sensitivity Scenarios with No Early Futility Stopping; Common Mortality
    and Function Effect
                  Scenario                             Null                       Alternative
                                                      DRR 1.0                      DRR 0.70
                                            Type I Error    Mean Est.       Power         Mean Est.
                                                              DRR                            DRR
    Base                                       0.024          1.01           0.77            0.69
    Slower Accrual                             0.025          1.01           0.77            0.69
    Faster Accrual                             0.020          1.01           0.76            0.69
    4% Dropout                                 0.024          1.01           0.74            0.69
    6% Dropout                                 0.024          1.01           0.71            0.69
    Reg. Start 3 Months Apart                  0.023          1.02           0.67            0.70
```

## Numeric definitions, units and schedules

### SP5-N001 — Treatment groups and allocation

[PDF p72](../../../joi240158supp2_prod_1742927563.7711.pdf#page=72), [p105](../../../joi240158supp2_prod_1742927563.7711.pdf#page=105), overview pp71/104: RGC CNM-Au8 30mg/day, 60mg/day and placebo. Within-regimen allocation 3:3:2, permuted blocks; stratification by baseline/master-screening riluzole/edaravone use (neither, riluzole only, edaravone only, both). Regimen assignment also uses those four strata. Approximately60 centers is administrative context; no planned randomized sample size or power input appears within this shard. Main matching keys: randomized intervention, dose groups, stratification, shared placebo.

### SP5-N002 — Dose formulation and tolerability

PDF pp72–73 and105–106: two60mL bottles/day; 30mg/day group15mg/bottle, concentration250µg/mL; 60mg/day group30mg/bottle, concentration500µg/mL; each formulation32.8mg sodium bicarbonate and60mL purified water. Down-titration to one bottle/day and rechallenge allowed with investigator/monitor approval; no drug holidays. First dose after all baseline assessments in clinic. 500µg/mL confirmed visually p106; native encoded concentration text retained in the copied locator. Matching key: actual randomized dose/duration and exposure.

### SP5-N003 — Schedule matrix

PDF pp73–74 and106–107. MP screening day−42 to−1; RGC screening day−41 to0; baseline day0; weeks2,4,8,12,16,20,24 at days14±3,28±7,56±7,84±3,112±7,140±3,168±7; final phone safety call28±3 days after last dose. Clinic: screening/baseline/weeks4,8,16,24; phone: weeks2,12,20/finalcall (weeks4/8/16 may be remote). Matrix p106 visually inspected: ALSFRS-R at MPscreening, baseline,4,8,12,16,20,24; SVC at MPscreening,baseline,8/16 if clinic,24; home spirometry at screening,baseline,8,16,24; strength baseline,8/16 if clinic,24; ALSAQ-40 baseline/24; CNS-BFS baseline,8,16,24. ECG screening/24; clinical labs screening,baseline,4,8,16,24. CNM PK/PD baseline,4/8 if clinic,24; biomarker blood and urine baseline,8/16 if clinic,24; optional DNA baseline; optional CSF week16 if clinic (footnote permits24); voice baseline,4,8,16,24; concomitant/AEs include screening and all scheduled followups, AEs include finalcall. Suicidality baseline,4,8,16,24. App installed baseline/uninstalled24. Regimen assignment MPscreening and treatment randomization baseline. Study drug/compliance and exit/vital-status matrix continue p107. Matching keys: measurement frequency,24week endpoints and followup windows.

### SP5-N004 — Schedule footnotes

PDF pp74–75/107–108. Dose held before4/8/24 visits. Wholeblood/urine only baseline and24/earlytermination despite broad collection rows; plasma sampling per visit; predose time, last meal and sample times recorded. Twiceweekly home voice recording, only one additional home recording in clinic-recording weeks. DNA may be collected later if baseline missing/unusable; CSF may shift16→24. Clinic-missingness due pandemic affects selected procedures, with remote ALSFRS-R, homeFVC/CNS-BFS8/16, voice, adverseevents and selected safety procedures. Vitalstatus obtained at individual end of blinded followup and again after all RGC blinded followups; later publicly available sources possible. Phone visits have compliance check but no drug accountability. Main matching keys: delayed ascertainment and pandemic-related missingness.

### SP5-N005 — ALSFRS-R change, delta-FRS and six-point endpoint

PDF pp77–78/111. delta-FRS=(48−baseline total)/months from symptomatic weakness onset to baseline. V3 specifies months=days×12/365.25 and onset-date imputation to15th if day unspecified. ALSFRS domains exploratory. Time to first≥6point decline forms a composite with death / death-equivalent, whicheverfirst; censor at last ALSFRS assessment through end of week24window. Subgroup labels on pp89/126 print delta-FRS thresholds in points/year; definition uses months. Both units are transcribed independently, without mapping diagnosis. Main matching keys: prebaseline progression covariate, ALSFRS clinical events and subgroup cutoffs.

### SP5-N006 — Respiratory measurement definitions

PDF pp78/111: age=assessment−birth days/365.25. GLI mapping: AmericanIndian/AlaskaNative→Mixed/Other; Asian→SouthEastAsian; Black/AfricanAmerican→AfricanAmerican; NativeHawaiian/OtherPacificIslander→Mixed/Other; White,Unknown,Notreported→Caucasian; multiplerace→Mixed/Other. HomeFVC uses maximum accepted of3–8 maneuvers and GLI norms using sex,age,height atscreening,race; higher value means betterfunction. Main keys: SVC percentpredicted, homeFVC.

### SP5-N007 — Strength event quantities

PDF pp78/112: HHD0 adds death / death-equivalent to masterdefinition; HHD02=second postbaseline occurrence of zero strength among muscles nonzero atbaseline, or death / death-equivalent whicheverfirst. Censor at lastHHD through24window. Upper/lower/globalHHD percentages andHHD0/02 exploratory. Main keys: muscle-strength endpoints, composite survival definitions.

### SP5-N008 — King's ALS stage thresholds

PDF pp79–80/113. Regions bulbar/upperlimb/lowerlimb define stages1–3;4a nutritional and4b respiratoryfailure. Bulbar: any questions1/2/3<4; upperlimb:4 or5A<4; lowerlimb:8<4; nutritionalfailure gastrostomy>50%nutrition; respiratoryfailure question10=0 or12<4. No region involvement still stage1 due diagnosis; may satisfy both4a/4b. Main keys: stageprogression exploratory event and stratification.

### SP5-N009 — Clinical events and PD collection

PDF pp80/114: time origin baseline; firstSAEhospitalization (electiveprocedures excluded), firstALS-relatedSAEhospitalization, assistedventilation, feedingtube, King's4a/4b, composite feedingtube/tracheostomy/PAV/death, and composite additionallySAEhospitalization. Everylisted event also includes death / death-equivalent. Applicable prevalent events excluded: existing ventilation/tube, baselineALSFRS5B, question10=0 or12<4. Censor week24visit, consentwithdrawal or lastknownstatus throughwindow; King's intervalcensored. Wholeblood/urinePD baseline/24; plasmaPD baseline/4/8/24. Main key: exploratory composites versus PAV-free survival.

### SP5-N010 — Riluzole PK sample definition

PDF pp81/114: first40 RGC riluzoleusers reachingweek8 sampled baseline/4/8; additional10 nonusers baseline. V1 planned HPLC ultraviolet263nm, assaymethodtobeconfirmed. V3 LC/MS/MS CovanceMethodM10125, study8454-985. Main key: safety/interaction PK analysis, not a clinical efficacy count.

### SP5-N011 — Exposure denominators

PDF pp92/129. ERO/SRO compliance; permanentdrug cessation date=lastuse ofblindeddrug. Exposuredays defined separately: (1) initiation→finalsafetyassessment inclusive; (2) initiation→withdrawal inclusive less recorded interruption intervals, not individualmisseddoses unlessdosagelog; (3) initiation→earlier finalblindedcontact or28daysafterlastdose inclusive. Proportions interrupting/reducing, timetofirst and reduceddoseexposuredays summarized. Main key: TEAE personyears/exposure and treatmentdiscontinuation.

### SP5-N012 — Version metadata

[PDF p94](../../../joi240158supp2_prod_1742927563.7711.pdf#page=94): R-SAP3.0 dated22July2022; masterprotocol4.0 dated31Aug2020; RSA5.0 dated22Nov2021; MasterSAP1.0 dated24June2020. History pp96–98 identifies v1.0 17March2022 andv2.0 9May2022. These are versionlocators, not result quantities.

## Full subgroup cutoffs and fallback rules (SP5-S029/SP5-S056)

[PDF p88](../../../joi240158supp2_prod_1742927563.7711.pdf#page=88), [p89](../../../joi240158supp2_prod_1742927563.7711.pdf#page=89), [p125](../../../joi240158supp2_prod_1742927563.7711.pdf#page=125), [p126](../../../joi240158supp2_prod_1742927563.7711.pdf#page=126). These supplement inherited M-SAP subgroups and use random-slopes FAS primary/secondary endpoint models:

- Riluzole/edaravone: neither, riluzole only, edaravone only, both.
- Age <65 vs≥65 years; sex female/male; race white vs any minority race with>5% prevalence; ethnicity Hispanic/Latino vsnon-Hispanic/Latino.
- Weight <43,43–<56,56–<77,77–<150,≥150kg.
- BMI <18.5,18.5–<25,25–<30,30–<40,≥40kg/m².
- CKD stage1orbetter eGFR≥90, stage2 60–89,stage3 30–59,stage4 15–29,stage5<15mL/min/1.73m².
- Weakness onset <18 vs≥18months; ElEscorialdefiniteand<18monthweakness versusnotboth.
- Baseline severity allALSFRSquestions≥2 vsany0/1.
- Earlydisease allALSFRSquestions≥2 ANDSVC≥80%predicted ANDsymptomonset<24months versusnotall.
- Serumurate <5.5 vs≥5.5mg/dL; baseline NfL mediansplit.
- Baseline delta-FRS <0.47,0.47–<1.11,≥1.11 points/year as printed in subgroup list, preserving units independently of monthlydefinition.
- Site individual if≥5participantseachtreatment group; poolsites belowthreshold.
- V3 additionally anybulbaronset versusnobulbaronset.

Unknown/notreported/missing combine as one group. Participantsoutside specifiedsubgroups formmixedother retainedonly if>5%prevalence. SafetyTEAEsubgroup otherretainedirrespectiveprevalence; safetyclassifications include riluzole,edaravone,age,sex,race,ethnicity,weight,BMI,CKD. Convergence fallback: regimenUN covariance→uncorrelatedregimenvariancecomponents→fixedregimenintercept/slopes→uncorrelatedparticipantvariancecomponents. This is a prespecified sequence; no alternative analysis is proposed.

## Equations and code, preserving printed names (SP5-S045/SP5-S047/SP5-S050/SP5-S055)

### Adjusted CAFS

[PDF p118](../../../joi240158supp2_prod_1742927563.7711.pdf#page=118) code:

```sas
proc mixed data=work.cafs;
class trtrnd(ref='0');
model Rank = sx2bl dFRS rlz edv trtrnd / solution clparm;
estimate "Active vs. Placebo (one-sided)" Treat 1 -1 / cl upper;
run;
```

Printed model variable `trtrnd` and printed estimate variable `Treat` are preserved, as is the one-sided label. Statistical screening is delegated to the checkers. Hierarchytext on the same page specifies two-tailed P<.05. This extraction records bothsource statements without adjudicating their relationship.

### Repeated-measures equation1 and SAS

[PDF p120](../../../joi240158supp2_prod_1742927563.7711.pdf#page=120), exact symbolreading checked in reusable renderedpage:

\[
Y_{ij}=a_{k(i)}+\gamma_1t_i+\gamma_{2,j}v_j+\boldsymbol\gamma_3'\mathbf z_i+
\gamma_{4,j}t_iv_j+\boldsymbol\gamma_{5,j}'\mathbf z_iv_j+\epsilon_{ij}.
\]

\(a_k\sim N(0,\sigma_r^2)\), \(\boldsymbol\epsilon_{i\cdot}\sim N(\mathbf0,\mathbf R)\); printed covariance line is \(\operatorname{Cov}(b_{k(i)},\epsilon_{ij})=0\). Prose describes covariance of regimen random interceptandresidual; source's `b` symbol is retained. \(Y\) endpoint; \(v\) visitindicator; \(z\) centeredonset time,deltaFRS,riluzole,edaravone; \(t\) treatmentindicator, prose/code use three groups; regimen random interceptvariance; withinparticipantunstructuredresidualmatrix. Prose says estimatedparameters; no Bayesian priors are specified for this frequentist supportive model.

```sas
proc mixed data=xxx method=reml;
class regimen id trtrnd visit;
model Value = trtrnd|visit
sx2bl|visit dFRS|visit rlz|visit edv|visit / solution cl;
random intercept / subject=regimen type=vc;
repeated visit / subject=id type=un;
```

24week change difference contrast on [p121](../../../joi240158supp2_prod_1742927563.7711.pdf#page=121):

```sas
estimate "3|Act vs Plb|dWk 24"
post*trtrnd*visit 2 0 0 -2   -1 0 0 1   -1 0 0 1 / cl divisor=2;
```

Time-since-onset and deltaFRS are centered atsamplemedian; baseline use indicators included. 95% Wald confidencebounds. The earlier v1shared baselinecode p85 has0atbaseline ineachblock. The v3revisionhistory explicitly discloses removalofshared baseline, while anestimandparagraph p120 retains shared-baseline wording; all occurrences are independentlylocated ratherthan normalized.

### Random-slopes equation2 and SAS

[PDF p122](../../../joi240158supp2_prod_1742927563.7711.pdf#page=122), rendered page inspected:

\[
Y_{ij}=\gamma_1+a^0_{k(i)}+b_i^0+\gamma_2t_i+\boldsymbol\gamma_3'\mathbf z_i+
(\gamma_4+a^1_{k(i)}+b_i^1+\gamma_5t_i+\boldsymbol\gamma_6'\mathbf z_i)m_{ij}+\epsilon_{ij}.
\]

Printed distribution lines: \(\{a_k^0,a_k^1\}\sim N(\mathbf0,\boldsymbol\Sigma_r)\), \(\{b_k^0,b_k^1\}\sim N(\mathbf0,\boldsymbol\Sigma_p)\), \(\epsilon_{ij}\sim N(0,\sigma_\epsilon^2)\); covariance ofregimen/participant/residual groups zero. Distribution line uses \(b_k\), equation/prose uses participant \(b_i\); retained printedindices. Bothrandom intercept/slopescovariances unstructured; residualvariance homogeneous. \(m_{ij}\)=daysfrombaseline×12/365.25; \(z\) samefour covariates.

```sas
proc mixed data=xxx method=reml;
class regimen id trtrnd;
model Value = trtrnd|month
sx2bl|month dFRS|month rlz|month edv|month / solution cl;
random intercept month / subject=regimen type=un;
random intercept month / subject=id type=un;
estimate "3|Act vs Plb|Slope (/mn)"
month 0 trtrnd*month -2 1 1 / cl divisor=2;
```

Last randomline andcontrast on [p123](../../../joi240158supp2_prod_1742927563.7711.pdf#page=123). Conditionalmeanslope difference,95% Wald CI; primary contrast pooled activedosesaveraged. Additionalclinicalbaseline NfL plusNfL×month.

### Placebo multiple imputation

[PDF p125](../../../joi240158supp2_prod_1742927563.7711.pdf#page=125):

```sas
proc mi data=work.outcomes seed=xxx nimpute=50 out=work.out_mi01
minimum=. . . . . x x x x maximum=. . . . . y y y y minmaxiter=1000;
class rlz edv trtrnd;
fcs reg(Wk08 = sx2bl dFRS rlz edv Wk00);
fcs reg(Wk16 = sx2bl dFRS rlz edv Wk00 Wk08);
fcs reg(Wk24 = sx2bl dFRS rlz edv Wk00 Wk08 Wk16);
mnar model( Wk08 Wk16 Wk24 / modelobs=(trtrnd="0"));
var sx2bl dFRS rlz edv Wk00 Wk08 Wk16 Wk24;
run;
```

Printed minimum/maximumlists andvarlist preserved withoutnormalizing their lengths. x/y=0/48ALSFRStotal,0/dotHHDpercentagesandSVC; trtrnd0placebo. Fiftyimputations, maximum1000bounditerations. Seed remainsplaceholder `xxx`; no numericseed is specifiedhere. Separatebaseline NfLcovariate inbothimputationstages, masterMI-analysispoolingdetails delegatedtootherSAPshard.

### DOC-004 PDF page 2

Source: [DOC-004 p.2](../../../joi240158supp3_prod_1742927563.7911.pdf#page=2). Relationship group SP6-N002; applicable statistical group as coverage table.

```text
                                                                                                                   1


eMethods



Trial Design and Oversight

The HEALEY ALS Platform Trial is a novel, efficient, perpetual design, platform trial designed to test multiple investigational

products against placebo in parallel. In this platform trial, each new investigational product is tested as a regimen specific appendix,

or “Regimen” to the master protocol. CNM-Au8 was evaluated in Regimen C (RGC) of the HEALEY ALS Platform Trial, a

randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial enrolled and followed participants at 54 Northeast ALS Consortium (NEALS)

centers in the United States between July 2020 and March 2022. The trial was conducted in accordance with Good Clinical Practice

guidelines of the International Conference on Harmonization and ethical principles of the Declaration of Helsinki. Protocol approval

was provided for all trial sites by a central Institutional Review Board, the Massachusetts General Brigham Human Research

Committee. All participants provided written informed consent prior to screening.



The trial was designed by and conducted through the NEALS network, a global collaborative trial network (www.neals.org), in

collaboration with Clene Nanomedicine, Inc. (Clene). Participant-level data were obtained at each trial site and stored in the electronic

data capture system for the trial at Massachusetts General Hospital. An independent Data and Safety Monitoring Board reviewed

unblinded safety data at pre-defined intervals throughout the trial. Statistical analyses were performed by biostatisticians at Berry
```

### DOC-004 PDF page 3

Source: [DOC-004 p.3](../../../joi240158supp3_prod_1742927563.7911.pdf#page=3). Relationship group SP6-N003; applicable statistical group as coverage table.

```text
                                                                                                                   2


Consultants, Massachusetts General Hospital, and Pentara Corporation. Clene provided the active drug and placebo and was involved

in the trial design, data analysis, and manuscript review.



Trial Participants

Those who were eligible for the platform trial were adults with a diagnosis of clinically possible, probable, lab-supported probable, or

definite ALS defined by revised El Escorial criteria, disease duration ≤ 36 months, a vital capacity ≥ 50% predicted for age, height,

and sex, the ability to swallow pills and liquids, and either no use of riluzole and/or edaravone or stable dosing of riluzole and/or

edaravone for more than 30 days or one cycle, respectively. Those eligible for the CNM-Au8 regimen could not be allergic to gold.

Full inclusion and exclusion criteria can be viewed in the trial protocol available as a Supplement.



Randomization techniques

Both the randomization to regimen, which was not blinded, and the randomization to active drug or placebo within a regimen, which

was blinded to both participant and investigator, were done using as pre-determined randomization schedule. In order to ensure

balance in regimens and treatment groups by current medication use, both randomization schedules were stratified by baseline use of

riluzole and edaravone (4 strata total).


Random assignment to a regimen was done. Random assignment to a regimen within a stratum uses a block urn design (Zhao and

Wang; Block urn design - a new randomization algorithm for sequential trials with two or more treatments and balanced or

                                                                                                                   2
```
